````markdown
# Research Brief: Carbon-Footprint Comparison of Everyday & Digital Activities

## 0. Mission (read first)

Produce a **referenced, order-of-magnitude comparison** of the energy use and greenhouse-gas emissions of 15 everyday, food and digital activities. The goal is that a non-expert reader can see whether a given AI/agent task is closer to a Google search, a hamburger, or a flight.

- **Precision target:** order of magnitude (×10). Do not spend time on decimal-level precision.
- **Every number must be traceable** to a reputable source, or to a calculation whose inputs are each sourced.
- **Make uncertainty visible:** give a *central estimate* plus a *low–high range* for each item.
- **Do not invent numbers.** If no good source exists, derive the value with the method in §4 and label it **"Derived"**.

---

## 1. Deliverables

| # | Artefact | Format | Description |
|---|----------|--------|-------------|
| D1 | **Research dossier** | Markdown | One section per item (template in §6): definition, assumptions, sources, calculation, result, caveats |
| D2 | **Comparison table** | Markdown table + CSV | Summary of D1 (columns in §7) |
| D3 | **Log-scale chart** *(optional, recommended)* | PNG/SVG | Horizontal bar chart of g CO₂e per functional unit on a log axis |
| D4 | **Bibliography** | Markdown list | Full references with URL, publisher, year and access date |
| D5 | **Assumptions register** | Markdown table | Every global and per-item assumption in one place, so a reader can challenge it |

---

## 2. Global conventions (apply to all items)

### 2.1 Units
- **Emissions:** grams CO₂-equivalent (**g CO₂e**). Switch to kg in the narrative where clearer. The table stays in g.
- **Energy:** report **both Wh/kWh and Joules** (1 Wh = 3,600 J; 1 kWh = 3.6 MJ).
- **Food:** "energy" is ambiguous (food calories vs. energy used in production). Report **CO₂e only** for food, put "n/a" in the energy column, and add a footnote. Optionally add food kcal as an extra column, clearly labelled.

### 2.2 System boundary
- **Primary figure = operational / use-phase emissions**, e.g. fuel burned, electricity used, data-centre + network + end-device energy.
- **Food = cradle-to-retail life-cycle emissions**, the standard in food LCA literature.
- **Embodied emissions** of devices, vehicles and servers: mention qualitatively. Add a separate number only if a reputable source makes it easy. Do not mix it into the primary figure.
- **Flights:** report **with and without** non-CO₂ effects (radiative forcing, RF). The table uses the value *with RF* and the footnote gives the other.

### 2.3 Electricity grid intensity (needed for all electricity-based items)
- **Reference grid:** Netherlands, latest full year (source: Ember, EEA or CBS). State the value used, e.g. "X g CO₂e/kWh (Ember, 2025)".
- **Data-centre loads** (search, streaming servers, LLM inference, CI runners): use the operator's published figure if one exists. Otherwise use a global or US average grid (IEA or Ember) and state it.
- **Sensitivity:** for electricity-based items, also show the result on a low-carbon grid (~50 g/kWh) and a coal-heavy grid (~700 g/kWh) in the dossier, not in the main table.
- **Market-based vs. location-based:** use location-based grid factors as the default. Mention company renewable-energy claims (Google, Microsoft, OpenAI/Azure, Anthropic/AWS/GCP) only as a caveat.

### 2.4 Data-centre overhead
- Apply a **PUE** (Power Usage Effectiveness) where server energy is estimated bottom-up. Use the operator's published PUE (Google, Microsoft/Azure) or the industry average from the Uptime Institute survey. State the value.

### 2.5 Source hierarchy (use the highest tier available)
1. Peer-reviewed papers and meta-analyses (e.g. *Science*, *Nature*, *Joule*, *Environmental Science & Technology*, arXiv papers with established authors, clearly flagged as preprints)
2. Intergovernmental and governmental bodies: IEA, EEA, IPCC, UK DESNZ/DEFRA GHG Conversion Factors, ICAO, US EPA, CBS/RIVM
3. Established NGOs and research groups: Our World in Data, Carbon Trust, The Shift Project (note that its 2019 streaming estimate was later criticised as too high), Ember, Epoch AI
4. Company disclosures: Google, Microsoft, OpenAI, Mistral, GitHub. Treat as self-reported.
5. Media and blogs: **only** to point to a tier 1–4 source, never as the source itself.

For contested items (streaming, LLM queries), **cite at least two independent sources** and explain why they differ.

### 2.6 Recency
Prefer sources from **2020 or later**, and from **2024 or later** for anything AI-related. Older figures, such as Google's 2009 per-search figure, may be used when nothing newer exists, but must be flagged with their year.

---

## 3. Items: definitions, functional units and default assumptions

> The agent may refine assumptions, but must record every change in the assumptions register (D5).

### Transport
| ID | Item | Functional unit | Default assumptions | Suggested starting sources |
|----|------|-----------------|---------------------|---------------------------|
| ✈️ | Flight Amsterdam → Geneva | 1 passenger, one way | Great-circle distance ≈ 700 km (verify); economy; direct; add uplift for actual flight path if the method requires | ICAO Carbon Emissions Calculator; DESNZ/DEFRA short-haul factors (with/without RF); EEA; myclimate/atmosfair methodology |
| ⛽️🚗 | Petrol car | 1 car, one way, **road distance** AMS→GVA (≈ 950–1,000 km; verify) | Average EU/NL petrol passenger car, real-world (not WLTP) consumption; 1 occupant. **Also give per-km and per-passenger (×1 and ×4 occupants) in the dossier** | DESNZ/DEFRA; EEA real-world CO₂ monitoring; ICCT |
| 🔋🚙 | Electric car | Same trip as ⛽️🚗 | Average EV real-world consumption (kWh/km incl. charging losses) × reference grid | ICCT; EEA; IEA Global EV Outlook; DESNZ/DEFRA |

> **Edge case — "same distance":** flight distance and road distance differ by ~35–40%. Use road distance for cars and flight distance for the flight. Also give **per-km** values for all three in the dossier so they can be compared like-for-like.

### Food
| ID | Item | Functional unit | Default assumptions | Suggested starting sources |
|----|------|-----------------|---------------------|---------------------------|
| 🍔 | Beef hamburger | 1 burger meal | ~113 g (¼ lb) beef patty + bun + garnish; fries/drink excluded (mention separately) | Poore & Nemecek (2018), *Science*, via Our World in Data; WRI |
| 🍗 | Chicken | 1 portion | ~150 g poultry meat | Same as above |
| 🧀 | Cheese | 1 portion | ~50 g cheese (state whether hard cheese) | Same as above; RIVM/Dutch food LCA databases (Agribalyse as cross-check) |

> Use the **global median** from Poore & Nemecek as the central value and the reported 10th–90th percentile as the range. Note that beef from beef herds differs strongly from beef from dairy herds.

### Everyday digital
| ID | Item | Functional unit | Default assumptions | Suggested starting sources |
|----|------|-----------------|---------------------|---------------------------|
| 🔍 | Google search (no AI) | 1 query | Server side + network + share of user device for ~a few seconds | Google (2009) 0.0003 kWh/search statement; newer independent estimates; note that AI Overviews change this |
| 📺 | Video streaming | 1 hour | HD on a laptop or TV over home Wi-Fi/fixed broadband; include device, network and data centre | IEA (Kamiya, 2020 revised analysis); Carbon Trust (2021) white paper on video streaming; note The Shift Project controversy |
| 🎧 | Music streaming | 1 hour | Smartphone or laptop, standard quality; include device, network and data centre | Peer-reviewed estimates if available (e.g. Brennan & Devine on recorded music); otherwise derive (§4.4) |
| 🧑‍💻 | Human computer work | 1 hour | Laptop (~15–30 W) + one external monitor (~20–30 W), local work only; **exclude** cloud services; embodied emissions as a caveat | Manufacturer product environmental reports (Dell, Apple, Lenovo), Energy Star data |
| 🧑‍🧑‍🧒‍🧒 | Teams meeting + recording | 1 meeting-hour | **4 participants**, cameras on, each on laptop + Wi-Fi; plus cloud recording stored for **1 year**. Report total per meeting and per participant | Obringer et al. (2021), *Resources, Conservation & Recycling*; Microsoft sustainability disclosures; derive storage via §4.4 |

### AI and compute
| ID | Item | Functional unit | Default assumptions | Suggested starting sources |
|----|------|-----------------|---------------------|---------------------------|
| 🤖 | Simple LLM chat query | 1 prompt + 1 response | Frontier chat model; ~100 input tokens, ~500 output tokens; no web search, no reasoning mode | Epoch AI (2025) ChatGPT energy estimate; Google (2025) Gemini per-prompt technical paper; Mistral LCA (2025); Jegham et al. (2025) "How Hungry is AI?"; Luccioni et al.; OpenAI CEO statement (flag as self-reported) |
| 🦾 | Agent task, medium | 1 task | GitHub Copilot agent / Claude Code; **~10 min wall-clock, ~10–30 tool calls**. Token profile to be researched (§4.2): expect most input tokens to be **cached context re-reads** | Derived from 🤖 per-token energy + token counts from public usage reports/telemetry (e.g. Claude Code `/cost` output examples, Anthropic/GitHub docs, developer write-ups) |
| 🦾🦾 | Agent task, large | 1 task | **~1 h wall-clock, 100+ tool calls**; same method | Same |
| 🗄️ | GitHub Actions CI | 5 min of runtime | Standard GitHub-hosted Linux runner (verify current vCPU/RAM spec for private vs. public repos); CPU-only; medium load (~50% utilisation) | GitHub runner specs; Cloud Carbon Footprint methodology (per-vCPU W, per-GB RAM W, PUE); Azure region grid intensity |

---

## 4. Calculation methods (use when no direct source exists)

### 4.1 General
```
Energy (Wh) = Power (W) × Time (h) × PUE (if data centre)
CO₂e (g)    = Energy (kWh) × Grid intensity (g CO₂e/kWh)
J           = Wh × 3,600
```

### 4.2 LLM / agent tasks
1. From the 🤖 sources, establish **energy per 1,000 output tokens** and, where available, **per 1,000 input tokens**. Input/prefill is much cheaper per token than output/decode, and **cached** input cheaper still. Document the ratio used.
2. Build a **token profile** for 🦾 and 🦾🦾:
   - number of model turns (≈ number of tool calls + 1)
   - context size per turn (grows over the session)
   - split into fresh input, cached input and output tokens
   - cross-check against real-world reported session token counts or costs. Price-to-token conversion is acceptable as a sanity check.
3. Energy = Σ(tokens × energy per token type) × PUE.
4. Add the **tool-execution side** (local laptop running tests, builds, etc.) using §4.1 and the 🧑‍💻 figure × duration.
5. Report a **low / central / high** scenario. The spread will be large; that is acceptable and should be stated.

### 4.3 CI runner
```
Power = vCPUs × (W_min + util × (W_max − W_min)) + RAM_GB × W_per_GB
Energy = Power × (5/60 h) × PUE
```
Use Cloud Carbon Footprint coefficients for Azure and state them.

### 4.4 Network and storage
- Network: use a **kWh/GB** figure from a recent peer-reviewed source (e.g. Aslan et al. 2018, or newer). Note that marginal network energy per GB is contested, and power-model approaches (Carbon Trust/IEA) give much lower values. State which approach is used.
- Storage, for the Teams recording: GB stored × kWh/GB·year (cite source) × replication factor.

---

## 5. Hypothesis ranges for sanity checks (NOT results)

Use these **only** to catch unit errors, e.g. a Wh/kWh mix-up. If a result falls outside the range, double-check it; if it holds, explain why.

| Item | Expected ballpark per functional unit |
|------|--------------------------------------|
| ✈️ AMS→GVA, 1 pax | ~100–250 kg |
| ⛽️🚗 ~1,000 km, 1 car | ~150–250 kg |
| 🔋🚙 ~1,000 km, NL grid | ~20–80 kg |
| 🍔 burger | ~3–12 kg |
| 🍗 chicken portion | ~0.5–2 kg |
| 🧀 cheese portion | ~0.5–2 kg |
| 🔍 search | ~0.1–1 g |
| 📺 1 h video | ~20–100 g |
| 🎧 1 h music | ~1–50 g |
| 🧑‍💻 1 h computer work | ~5–30 g |
| 🧑‍🧑‍🧒‍🧒 1 h Teams (4 people) | ~50–500 g |
| 🤖 chat query | ~0.03–5 g |
| 🦾 / 🦾🦾 agent tasks | unknown: key research gap, expect 1–3 orders of magnitude above 🤖 |
| 🗄️ 5 min CI | ~0.5–5 g |

---

## 6. Dossier template (one section per item, D1)

```markdown
### [Emoji] [Item name]
**Functional unit:** …
**Boundary:** operational / life-cycle / with-or-without RF
**Assumptions:** bullet list (also copied to D5)
**Sources:**
1. [Author/Org, Year. Title. Publisher. URL. Accessed YYYY-MM-DD] — tier X — key figure quoted verbatim
2. …
**Calculation:** step-by-step with units
**Result:** central X g CO₂e (range Y–Z) | energy A Wh = B J
**Confidence:** High / Medium / Low, with one-line justification
**Caveats & disagreements:** …
```

---

## 7. Comparison table spec (D2)

Sort rows by **central g CO₂e per functional unit, descending**.

| Column | Content |
|--------|---------|
| Item | Emoji + short label |
| Functional unit | e.g. "1 pax, AMS→GVA one-way", "1 hour", "1 query" |
| Absolute rate | Intensity in the natural unit, e.g. g CO₂e/km, kg CO₂e/kg food, W or Wh/hour, Wh/1k tokens |
| Energy per unit | Wh **and** J (n/a for food) |
| CO₂e per unit, central | g CO₂e |
| CO₂e range | low–high |
| Order of magnitude | 10ⁿ g |
| Equivalent in 🤖 queries | central ÷ 🤖 central (an intuitive yardstick) |
| Confidence | H / M / L |
| Key source(s) | Short citation keys pointing to D4 |

Below the table, add:
- 3–5 **key takeaways** in plain language, e.g. "one burger ≈ N agent tasks"
- The **grid-intensity and RF assumptions** in one line each

---

## 8. Workflow for the agent

1. **Set the global parameters** in §2 (grid intensity, PUE, RF factor) and record them in D5.
2. **Research the items in the order of §3.** For each one, follow the dossier template. Expect transport and food to be quick; digital and AI items take the most effort.
3. **Run the calculations** from §4 where needed. Show the working and keep a spreadsheet or script so the numbers can be reproduced.
4. **Check results against the ranges in §5** and investigate any outliers.
5. **Compile D2–D4** from the dossier.
6. **Self-review** against the acceptance checklist (§9).

---

## 9. Acceptance checklist

- [ ] All 15 items present with central value + range
- [ ] Every number has a cited source or a derivation whose inputs are all cited
- [ ] At least two sources for 📺, 🤖, 🔍, ✈️
- [ ] Grid intensity, PUE and RF assumptions stated once globally and applied consistently
- [ ] Units consistent (g CO₂e; Wh and J); no Wh/kWh or kg/g mix-ups
- [ ] Per-km values given for all transport modes; occupancy stated for cars
- [ ] Food reported on a life-cycle basis, with this noted in the table
- [ ] Agent-task token profile documented and cross-checked
- [ ] Self-reported company figures flagged as such
- [ ] All URLs resolve; access dates recorded
- [ ] Takeaways are proportionate and do not overstate precision

---

## 10. Out of scope

- Water use, land use and biodiversity: may be mentioned in one line where striking (e.g. beef, AI data-centre water), but not quantified
- Model **training** emissions: mention as a caveat only, possibly as an amortised-per-query figure if a reputable source offers one
- Offsetting and carbon-credit claims
- Precision beyond the order of magnitude
````
:::

**Decisions I made that you may want to change:**
- **"Same distance" for the cars:** I used the actual road distance, about 1,000 km, because the flight distance is only about 700 km. The brief also asks for per-km values for all three modes, so they can be compared directly.
- **Portion sizes:** I set them to a 113 g (¼ lb) beef patty, 150 g of chicken and 50 g of cheese.
- **Teams meeting:** the default is 4 participants with cameras on and a recording kept for 1 year.
- **Energy column for food:** food gets CO₂e only, with "n/a" for energy. Food calories and the energy used to produce food would be confusing in one column.
- **The two agent tasks:** they're the hardest rows, because there's no published per-task figure. The brief has the agent build an estimate from three things: energy per token, a typical token count per task, and the laptop's own energy while tools run. It reports a low, central and high scenario.
- **Expected ranges in §5:** these are my own rough estimates, not sourced figures. They're labelled "not results" and are only meant to catch unit mistakes. Delete that section if you'd rather the agent not be anchored by them.

````markdown
# Research Brief: Carbon-Footprint Comparison of Everyday & Digital Activities

## 0. Mission (read first)

Produce a **referenced, order-of-magnitude comparison** of the **energy use** and **greenhouse-gas emissions** of 15 everyday, food and digital activities. The goal is that a non-expert reader can see whether a given AI/agent task is closer to a Google search, a hamburger, or a flight.

- **Precision target:** order of magnitude (×10). Do not spend time on decimal-level precision.
- **Every number must be traceable** to a reputable source, or to a calculation whose inputs are each sourced.
- **Make uncertainty visible:** give a *central estimate* plus a *low–high range* for each item.
- **Every item gets both an energy figure and a CO₂e figure in the same units**, so rows can be compared directly. This includes food, where energy means the energy used to produce it.
- **Do not invent numbers.** If no good source exists, derive the value with the method in §4 and label it **"Derived"**.

---

## 1. Deliverables

| # | Artefact | Format | Description |
|---|----------|--------|-------------|
| D1 | **Research dossier** | Markdown | One section per item (template in §6): definition, assumptions, sources, calculation, result, caveats |
| D2 | **Comparison table** | Markdown table + CSV | Summary of D1 (columns in §7) |
| D3 | **Log-scale charts** *(optional, recommended)* | PNG/SVG | Two horizontal bar charts on log axes: (a) g CO₂e per functional unit, (b) Wh per functional unit |
| D4 | **Bibliography** | Markdown list | Full references with URL, publisher, year and access date |
| D5 | **Assumptions register** | Markdown table | Every global and per-item assumption in one place, so a reader can challenge it |
| D6 | **Footnote: footprint of this report** *(nice-to-have)* | Short Markdown section at the end of the report | Estimated energy and CO₂e needed to produce this report (§4.5) |

---

## 2. Global conventions (apply to all items)

### 2.1 Units
- **Emissions:** grams CO₂-equivalent (**g CO₂e**). Switch to kg in the narrative where clearer. The table stays in g.
- **Energy:** report **both Wh and Joules** for every item (1 Wh = 3,600 J; 1 kWh = 3.6 MJ). The table stays in Wh; use kWh/MJ in the narrative where clearer.
- **Same units for all rows:** every row, including food, must have a value in both the Wh/J and the g CO₂e columns, so rows can be compared directly.

### 2.2 Energy definitions per category
Different categories use different energy bases. Label the basis in every row (column "Energy basis" in §7):

| Category | Energy definition | Basis label |
|----------|-------------------|-------------|
| Transport | Fuel or electricity used by the vehicle (tank-to-wheel; for EVs, at the plug, incl. charging losses) | **Final** |
| Digital | Electricity used by end device + network + data centre (incl. PUE) | **Final** |
| Food | **Production energy only**: cumulative energy demand (CED) from farm to retail, i.e. fuel, electricity, fertiliser manufacture, feed, processing, transport, refrigeration. **Food calories (kcal) are not used anywhere.** | **Primary (CED)** |

**Comparability rule:** food LCA sources report *primary* energy, while the transport and digital rows are *final* energy. To keep the rows comparable:
- add a second energy column, **"Primary energy (Wh)"**, for **all** rows;
- convert electricity to primary energy with a stated primary-energy factor (e.g. the EU/NL default, ~1.9–2.5; cite the source and value used);
- convert fuels to primary energy with a stated well-to-tank uplift (e.g. from JEC well-to-wheels or DESNZ/DEFRA WTT factors).
The main comparison uses primary energy; final energy stays in the table for transparency.

**Expected insight to explain in the report:** for food, CO₂e and energy are only loosely related, because most food emissions are not from energy use. They are methane (cattle), nitrous oxide (fertiliser, manure) and land-use change. Beef will therefore look much worse on CO₂e than on energy. Show this gap and explain it briefly.

### 2.3 System boundary
- **Primary figure = operational / use-phase emissions**, e.g. fuel burned, electricity used, data-centre + network + end-device energy.
- **Food = cradle-to-retail life-cycle emissions and energy**, the standard in food LCA literature. Exclude home cooking. Mention it in one line, with a rough figure if one is easy to source.
- **Embodied emissions** of devices, vehicles and servers: mention qualitatively. Add a separate number only if a reputable source makes it easy. Do not mix it into the primary figure.
- **Flights:** report **with and without** non-CO₂ effects (radiative forcing, RF). The table uses the value *with RF* and the footnote gives the other. RF affects CO₂e only, not energy.

### 2.4 Electricity grid intensity (needed for all electricity-based items)
- **Reference grid:** Netherlands, latest full year (source: Ember, EEA or CBS). State the value used, e.g. "X g CO₂e/kWh (Ember, 2025)".
- **Data-centre loads** (search, streaming servers, LLM inference, CI runners): use the operator's published figure if one exists. Otherwise use a global or US average grid (IEA or Ember) and state it.
- **Sensitivity:** for electricity-based items, also show the result on a low-carbon grid (~50 g/kWh) and a coal-heavy grid (~700 g/kWh) in the dossier, not in the main table.
- **Market-based vs. location-based:** use location-based grid factors as the default. Mention company renewable-energy claims (Google, Microsoft, OpenAI/Azure, Anthropic/AWS/GCP) only as a caveat.

### 2.5 Data-centre overhead
- Apply a **PUE** (Power Usage Effectiveness) where server energy is estimated bottom-up. Use the operator's published PUE (Google, Microsoft/Azure) or the industry average from the Uptime Institute survey. State the value.

### 2.6 Source hierarchy (use the highest tier available)
1. Peer-reviewed papers and meta-analyses (e.g. *Science*, *Nature*, *Joule*, *Environmental Science & Technology*, *Journal of Cleaner Production*, arXiv papers with established authors, clearly flagged as preprints)
2. Intergovernmental and governmental bodies and public LCA databases: IEA, EEA, IPCC, UK DESNZ/DEFRA GHG Conversion Factors, ICAO, US EPA, CBS/RIVM, **Agribalyse (ADEME)**, JEC Well-to-Wheels (EU JRC)
3. Established NGOs and research groups: Our World in Data, Carbon Trust, The Shift Project (note that its 2019 streaming estimate was later criticised as too high), Ember, Epoch AI
4. Company disclosures: Google, Microsoft, OpenAI, Mistral, GitHub, Anthropic. Treat as self-reported.
5. Media and blogs: **only** to point to a tier 1–4 source, never as the source itself.

For contested items (streaming, LLM queries), **cite at least two independent sources** and explain why they differ.

### 2.7 Recency
Prefer sources from **2020 or later**, and from **2024 or later** for anything AI-related. Older figures, such as Google's 2009 per-search figure, may be used when nothing newer exists, but must be flagged with their year.

---

## 3. Items: definitions, functional units and default assumptions

> The agent may refine assumptions, but must record every change in the assumptions register (D5).

### Transport
| ID | Item | Functional unit | Default assumptions | Suggested starting sources |
|----|------|-----------------|---------------------|---------------------------|
| ✈️ | Flight Amsterdam → Geneva | 1 passenger, one way | Great-circle distance ≈ 700 km (verify); economy; direct; add uplift for actual flight path if the method requires. Energy = jet fuel per passenger × ~12 kWh/kg kerosene (cite LHV) | ICAO Carbon Emissions Calculator (fuel burn per pax); DESNZ/DEFRA short-haul factors (with/without RF); EEA; myclimate/atmosfair methodology |
| ⛽️🚗 | Petrol car | 1 car, one way, **road distance** AMS→GVA (≈ 950–1,000 km; verify) | Average EU/NL petrol passenger car, real-world (not WLTP) consumption; 1 occupant. Energy = litres × ~8.9 kWh/L petrol (cite LHV). **Also give per-km and per-passenger (×1 and ×4 occupants) in the dossier** | DESNZ/DEFRA; EEA real-world CO₂ monitoring; ICCT; JEC WTW |
| 🔋🚙 | Electric car | Same trip as ⛽️🚗 | Average EV real-world consumption (kWh/km incl. charging losses) × reference grid | ICCT; EEA; IEA Global EV Outlook; DESNZ/DEFRA |

> **Edge case — "same distance":** flight distance and road distance differ by ~35–40%. Use road distance for cars and flight distance for the flight. Also give **per-km** values (Wh/km and g CO₂e/km) for all three in the dossier so they can be compared like-for-like.

### Food
| ID | Item | Functional unit | Default assumptions | Suggested starting sources |
|----|------|-----------------|---------------------|---------------------------|
| 🍔 | Beef hamburger | 1 burger meal | ~113 g (¼ lb) beef patty + bun + garnish; fries/drink excluded (mention separately) | **CO₂e:** Poore & Nemecek (2018), *Science*, via Our World in Data; WRI. **Energy (CED):** Agribalyse 3.x indicator "energy resources, non-renewable" (MJ/kg); peer-reviewed beef LCAs reporting CED; ecoinvent-based studies |
| 🍗 | Chicken | 1 portion | ~150 g poultry meat | Same as above |
| 🧀 | Cheese | 1 portion | ~50 g cheese (state whether hard cheese) | Same as above; RIVM/Dutch food LCA database as cross-check |

> - **CO₂e:** use the **global median** from Poore & Nemecek as the central value and the reported 10th–90th percentile as the range. Note that beef from beef herds differs strongly from beef from dairy herds.
> - **Energy:** report **production energy only** (CED, MJ/kg → Wh per portion). If CED for the exact product is unavailable, use the closest product and say so. If sources mix renewable and non-renewable CED, use **total CED** and note it.
> - **Do not report or mention food calories.**
> - CO₂e and energy may come from different studies. Note this, and check that the system boundaries roughly match (cradle-to-retail).

### Everyday digital
| ID | Item | Functional unit | Default assumptions | Suggested starting sources |
|----|------|-----------------|---------------------|---------------------------|
| 🔍 | Google search (no AI) | 1 query | Server side + network + share of user device for ~a few seconds | Google (2009) 0.0003 kWh/search statement; newer independent estimates; note that AI Overviews change this |
| 📺 | Video streaming | 1 hour | HD on a laptop or TV over home Wi-Fi/fixed broadband; include device, network and data centre | IEA (Kamiya, 2020 revised analysis); Carbon Trust (2021) white paper on video streaming; note The Shift Project controversy |
| 🎧 | Music streaming | 1 hour | Smartphone or laptop, standard quality; include device, network and data centre | Peer-reviewed estimates if available (e.g. Brennan & Devine on recorded music); otherwise derive (§4.4) |
| 🧑‍💻 | Human computer work | 1 hour | Laptop (~15–30 W) + one external monitor (~20–30 W), local work only; **exclude** cloud services; embodied emissions as a caveat | Manufacturer product environmental reports (Dell, Apple, Lenovo), Energy Star data |
| 🧑‍🧑‍🧒‍🧒 | Teams meeting + recording | 1 meeting-hour | **4 participants**, cameras on, each on laptop + Wi-Fi; plus cloud recording stored for **1 year**. Report total per meeting and per participant | Obringer et al. (2021), *Resources, Conservation & Recycling*; Microsoft sustainability disclosures; derive storage via §4.4 |

### AI and compute
| ID | Item | Functional unit | Default assumptions | Suggested starting sources |
|----|------|-----------------|---------------------|---------------------------|
| 🤖 | Simple LLM chat query | 1 prompt + 1 response | Frontier chat model; ~100 input tokens, ~500 output tokens; no web search, no reasoning mode | Epoch AI (2025) ChatGPT energy estimate; Google (2025) Gemini per-prompt technical paper; Mistral LCA (2025); Jegham et al. (2025) "How Hungry is AI?"; Luccioni et al.; OpenAI CEO statement (flag as self-reported) |
| 🦾 | Agent task, medium | 1 task | GitHub Copilot agent / Claude Code; **~10 min wall-clock, ~10–30 tool calls**. Token profile to be researched (§4.2): expect most input tokens to be **cached context re-reads** | Derived from 🤖 per-token energy + token counts from public usage reports/telemetry (e.g. Claude Code `/cost` output examples, Anthropic/GitHub docs, developer write-ups) |
| 🦾🦾 | Agent task, large | 1 task | **~1 h wall-clock, 100+ tool calls**; same method | Same |
| 🗄️ | GitHub Actions CI | 5 min of runtime | Standard GitHub-hosted Linux runner (verify current vCPU/RAM spec for private vs. public repos); CPU-only; medium load (~50% utilisation) | GitHub runner specs; Cloud Carbon Footprint methodology (per-vCPU W, per-GB RAM W, PUE); Azure region grid intensity |

---

## 4. Calculation methods (use when no direct source exists)

### 4.1 General
```
Energy (Wh) = Power (W) × Time (h) × PUE (if data centre)
CO₂e (g)    = Energy (kWh) × Grid intensity (g CO₂e/kWh)
J           = Wh × 3,600
Primary energy (Wh) = Final electricity (Wh) × primary-energy factor
                    = Final fuel energy (Wh) × (1 + well-to-tank uplift)
Food: Energy (Wh per portion) = CED (MJ/kg) × portion (kg) × 1,000,000 / 3,600
```

### 4.2 LLM / agent tasks
1. From the 🤖 sources, establish **energy per 1,000 output tokens** and, where available, **per 1,000 input tokens**. Input/prefill is much cheaper per token than output/decode, and **cached** input cheaper still. Document the ratio used.
2. Build a **token profile** for 🦾 and 🦾🦾:
   - number of model turns (≈ number of tool calls + 1)
   - context size per turn (grows over the session)
   - split into fresh input, cached input and output tokens
   - cross-check against real-world reported session token counts or costs. Price-to-token conversion is acceptable as a sanity check.
3. Energy = Σ(tokens × energy per token type) × PUE.
4. Add the **tool-execution side** (local laptop running tests, builds, etc.) using §4.1 and the 🧑‍💻 figure × duration.
5. Report a **low / central / high** scenario. The spread will be large; that is acceptable and should be stated.

### 4.3 CI runner
```
Power = vCPUs × (W_min + util × (W_max − W_min)) + RAM_GB × W_per_GB
Energy = Power × (5/60 h) × PUE
```
Use Cloud Carbon Footprint coefficients for Azure and state them.

### 4.4 Network and storage
- Network: use a **kWh/GB** figure from a recent peer-reviewed source (e.g. Aslan et al. 2018, or newer). Note that marginal network energy per GB is contested, and power-model approaches (Carbon Trust/IEA) give much lower values. State which approach is used.
- Storage, for the Teams recording: GB stored × kWh/GB·year (cite source) × replication factor.

### 4.5 Footprint of this report (D6, nice-to-have)
**Log from the very start of the work.** This cannot be reconstructed reliably afterwards.

1. **Record during the work:**
   - total model turns / LLM calls
   - input, cached-input and output tokens, read from the agent's usage/cost metadata where available, otherwise estimated
   - number of tool calls, split by type (web searches, page fetches, code runs)
   - total wall-clock time of the agent session
   - human time spent briefing and reviewing (estimate, in hours)
2. **Calculate:**
   - LLM inference: same per-token method and factors as 🦾/🦾🦾 (§4.2)
   - web searches: × 🔍 figure (or 🤖 figure if the searches are AI-assisted; state which)
   - code execution / sandbox: §4.1 with an assumed small VM power, × duration
   - human computer time: × 🧑‍💻 figure
3. **Report:** low / central / high for energy (Wh) and CO₂e (g), plus the token counts and tool-call counts used. Add one comparison sentence, e.g. "producing this report ≈ N Google searches ≈ X % of one burger".
4. **Place it as the last footnote of the report**, clearly labelled as a self-estimate using the report's own method.

---

## 5. Hypothesis ranges for sanity checks (NOT results)

Use these **only** to catch unit errors, e.g. a Wh/kWh mix-up. If a result falls outside the range, double-check it; if it holds, explain why. Energy ranges are **final energy** for transport/digital and **production energy (CED)** for food.

| Item | Expected energy ballpark | Expected CO₂e ballpark |
|------|-------------------------|-----------------------|
| ✈️ AMS→GVA, 1 pax | ~150–400 kWh | ~100–250 kg |
| ⛽️🚗 ~1,000 km, 1 car | ~500–800 kWh | ~150–250 kg |
| 🔋🚙 ~1,000 km | ~150–250 kWh | ~20–80 kg (NL grid) |
| 🍔 burger | ~0.5–5 kWh | ~3–12 kg |
| 🍗 chicken portion | ~0.5–3 kWh | ~0.5–2 kg |
| 🧀 cheese portion | ~0.3–2 kWh | ~0.5–2 kg |
| 🔍 search | ~0.1–1 Wh | ~0.1–1 g |
| 📺 1 h video | ~20–200 Wh | ~20–100 g |
| 🎧 1 h music | ~2–50 Wh | ~1–50 g |
| 🧑‍💻 1 h computer work | ~30–70 Wh | ~5–30 g |
| 🧑‍🧑‍🧒‍🧒 1 h Teams (4 people) | ~150–600 Wh | ~50–500 g |
| 🤖 chat query | ~0.1–5 Wh | ~0.03–5 g |
| 🦾 / 🦾🦾 agent tasks | unknown: key research gap | expect 1–3 orders of magnitude above 🤖 |
| 🗄️ 5 min CI | ~1–10 Wh | ~0.5–5 g |
| 📄 this report (D6) | unknown | expect roughly the 🦾🦾 range or higher |

---

## 6. Dossier template (one section per item, D1)

```markdown
### [Emoji] [Item name]
**Functional unit:** …
**Boundary:** operational / life-cycle / with-or-without RF
**Energy basis:** Final / Primary (CED); primary-energy factor used: …
**Assumptions:** bullet list (also copied to D5)
**Sources:**
1. [Author/Org, Year. Title. Publisher. URL. Accessed YYYY-MM-DD] — tier X — key figure quoted verbatim
2. …
**Calculation:** step-by-step with units
**Result:**
- Energy: central A Wh = B J (range …); primary energy C Wh
- CO₂e: central X g (range Y–Z)
**Confidence:** High / Medium / Low, with one-line justification
**Caveats & disagreements:** …
```

---

## 7. Comparison table spec (D2)

Sort rows by **central g CO₂e per functional unit, descending**.

| Column | Content |
|--------|---------|
| Item | Emoji + short label |
| Functional unit | e.g. "1 pax, AMS→GVA one-way", "1 burger", "1 hour", "1 query" |
| Absolute rate | Intensity in the natural unit, e.g. Wh/km and g CO₂e/km; MJ/kg and kg CO₂e/kg food; W; Wh/1k tokens |
| Energy basis | Final / Primary (CED) |
| Energy per unit (final) | Wh **and** J (for food: production CED; marked) |
| Energy per unit (primary) | Wh: the like-for-like comparison column for **all** rows |
| CO₂e per unit, central | g CO₂e |
| CO₂e range | low–high |
| Order of magnitude | 10ⁿ g CO₂e and 10ⁿ Wh |
| Equivalent in 🤖 queries | central CO₂e ÷ 🤖 central CO₂e (an intuitive yardstick) |
| Confidence | H / M / L |
| Key source(s) | Short citation keys pointing to D4 |

Below the table, add:
- 3–5 **key takeaways** in plain language, e.g. "one burger ≈ N agent tasks", and the energy-vs-CO₂e gap for food
- The **grid-intensity, primary-energy-factor and RF assumptions** in one line each
- **Last footnote:** the footprint of this report (D6)

---

## 8. Workflow for the agent

0. **Start logging your own usage** (tokens, tool calls, time) for D6 (§4.5).
1. **Set the global parameters** in §2 (grid intensity, PUE, RF factor, primary-energy factors) and record them in D5.
2. **Research the items in the order of §3.** For each one, follow the dossier template. Expect transport and food CO₂e to be quick; food energy (CED) and the digital and AI items take the most effort.
3. **Run the calculations** from §4 where needed. Show the working and keep a spreadsheet or script so the numbers can be reproduced.
4. **Check results against the ranges in §5** and investigate any outliers.
5. **Compile D2–D5** from the dossier.
6. **Close the usage log and compute D6.** Add it as the last footnote.
7. **Self-review** against the acceptance checklist (§9).

---

## 9. Acceptance checklist

- [ ] All 15 items present with central value + range, for **both energy and CO₂e**
- [ ] Food energy = production energy (CED) only; no food calories anywhere
- [ ] Energy basis (final / primary) labelled per row; primary-energy column filled for all rows with factors stated
- [ ] Every number has a cited source or a derivation whose inputs are all cited
- [ ] At least two sources for 📺, 🤖, 🔍, ✈️
- [ ] Grid intensity, PUE, primary-energy factors and RF assumptions stated once globally and applied consistently
- [ ] Units consistent (g CO₂e; Wh and J); no Wh/kWh, kg/g or MJ/kWh mix-ups
- [ ] Per-km values given for all transport modes; occupancy stated for cars
- [ ] Food reported on a life-cycle basis, with this noted in the table
- [ ] Agent-task token profile documented and cross-checked
- [ ] Self-reported company figures flagged as such
- [ ] All URLs resolve; access dates recorded
- [ ] Takeaways are proportionate and do not overstate precision
- [ ] *(Nice-to-have)* D6 footprint-of-this-report footnote included, with the token and tool-call counts it is based on

---

## 10. Out of scope

- Food calories / nutritional energy
- Water use, land use and biodiversity: may be mentioned in one line where striking (e.g. beef, AI data-centre water), but not quantified
- Model **training** emissions: mention as a caveat only, possibly as an amortised-per-query figure if a reputable source offers one
- Offsetting and carbon-credit claims
- Precision beyond the order of magnitude
````

- **Same units for every row:** every row now has a value in Wh and J and in g CO₂e.
  - Food sources report the total energy behind production ("primary energy"), while the car and digital rows measure only the electricity or fuel used directly. So I added a "primary energy" column for all rows, using stated conversion factors. That column is the one to compare across categories.

````markdown
# Research Brief: Carbon-Footprint Comparison of Everyday & Digital Activities

## 0. Mission (read first)

Produce a **referenced, order-of-magnitude comparison** of the **energy use** and **greenhouse-gas emissions** of 16 everyday, transport, logistics, food and digital activities. The goal is that a non-expert reader can see whether a given AI/agent task is closer to a Google search, a parcel delivery, a hamburger, or a flight.

- **Precision target:** order of magnitude (×10). Do not spend time on decimal-level precision.
- **Every number must be traceable** to a reputable source, or to a calculation whose inputs are each sourced.
- **Make uncertainty visible:** give a *central estimate* plus a *low–high range* for each item.
- **Every item gets both an energy figure and a CO₂e figure in the same units**, so rows can be compared directly. This includes food, where energy means the energy used to produce it.
- **Do not invent numbers.** If no good source exists, derive the value with the method in §4 and label it **"Derived"**.

---

## 1. Deliverables

| # | Artefact | Format | Description |
|---|----------|--------|-------------|
| D1 | **Research dossier** | Markdown | One section per item (template in §6): definition, assumptions, sources, calculation, result, caveats |
| D2 | **Comparison table** | Markdown table + CSV | Summary of D1 (columns in §7) |
| D3 | **Log-scale charts** *(optional, recommended)* | PNG/SVG | Two horizontal bar charts on log axes: (a) g CO₂e per functional unit, (b) Wh per functional unit |
| D4 | **Bibliography** | Markdown list | Full references with URL, publisher, year and access date |
| D5 | **Assumptions register** | Markdown table | Every global and per-item assumption in one place, so a reader can challenge it |
| D6 | **Footnote: footprint of this report** *(nice-to-have)* | Short Markdown section at the end of the report | Estimated energy and CO₂e needed to produce this report (§4.6) |

---

## 2. Global conventions (apply to all items)

### 2.1 Units
- **Emissions:** grams CO₂-equivalent (**g CO₂e**). Switch to kg in the narrative where clearer. The table stays in g.
- **Energy:** report **both Wh and Joules** for every item (1 Wh = 3,600 J; 1 kWh = 3.6 MJ). The table stays in Wh; use kWh/MJ in the narrative where clearer.
- **Same units for all rows:** every row, including food, must have a value in both the Wh/J and the g CO₂e columns, so rows can be compared directly.

### 2.2 Energy definitions per category
Different categories use different energy bases. Label the basis in every row (column "Energy basis" in §7):

| Category | Energy definition | Basis label |
|----------|-------------------|-------------|
| Transport | Fuel or electricity used by the vehicle (tank-to-wheel; for EVs, at the plug, incl. charging losses) | **Final** |
| Logistics | Fuel/electricity of all vehicles in the delivery chain (tank-to-wheel / at the plug) **plus** electricity and heating of warehouse, sorting hub and depot, allocated per parcel | **Final** |
| Digital | Electricity used by end device + network + data centre (incl. PUE) | **Final** |
| Food | **Production energy only**: cumulative energy demand (CED) from farm to retail, i.e. fuel, electricity, fertiliser manufacture, feed, processing, transport, refrigeration. **Food calories (kcal) are not used anywhere.** | **Primary (CED)** |

**Comparability rule:** food LCA sources report *primary* energy, while the transport, logistics and digital rows are *final* energy. To keep the rows comparable:
- add a second energy column, **"Primary energy (Wh)"**, for **all** rows;
- convert electricity to primary energy with a stated primary-energy factor (e.g. the EU/NL default, ~1.9–2.5; cite the source and value used);
- convert fuels to primary energy with a stated well-to-tank uplift (e.g. from JEC well-to-wheels or DESNZ/DEFRA WTT factors).
The main comparison uses primary energy; final energy stays in the table for transparency.

**Expected insight to explain in the report:** for food, CO₂e and energy are only loosely related, because most food emissions are not from energy use. They are methane (cattle), nitrous oxide (fertiliser, manure) and land-use change. Beef will therefore look much worse on CO₂e than on energy. Show this gap and explain it briefly.

### 2.3 System boundary
- **Primary figure = operational / use-phase emissions**, e.g. fuel burned, electricity used, data-centre + network + end-device energy.
- **Food = cradle-to-retail life-cycle emissions and energy**, the standard in food LCA literature. Exclude home cooking. Mention it in one line, with a rough figure if one is easy to source.
- **Logistics = operational emissions of the delivery chain only** (warehouse → linehaul → sorting/depot → last mile → door, incl. failed delivery attempts). **Exclude** production of the product **and** of the packaging material.
- **Embodied emissions** of devices, vehicles, buildings and servers: mention qualitatively. Add a separate number only if a reputable source makes it easy. Do not mix it into the primary figure.
- **Flights:** report **with and without** non-CO₂ effects (radiative forcing, RF). The table uses the value *with RF* and the footnote gives the other. RF affects CO₂e only, not energy.

### 2.4 Electricity grid intensity (needed for all electricity-based items)
- **Reference grid:** Netherlands, latest full year (source: Ember, EEA or CBS). State the value used, e.g. "X g CO₂e/kWh (Ember, 2025)". Also use this grid for the EV car, electric delivery vans and the NL warehouse/hub.
- **Data-centre loads** (search, streaming servers, LLM inference, CI runners): use the operator's published figure if one exists. Otherwise use a global or US average grid (IEA or Ember) and state it.
- **Sensitivity:** for electricity-based items, also show the result on a low-carbon grid (~50 g/kWh) and a coal-heavy grid (~700 g/kWh) in the dossier, not in the main table.
- **Market-based vs. location-based:** use location-based grid factors as the default. Mention company renewable-energy claims (Google, Microsoft, OpenAI/Azure, Anthropic/AWS/GCP, PostNL/DHL) only as a caveat.

### 2.5 Data-centre overhead
- Apply a **PUE** (Power Usage Effectiveness) where server energy is estimated bottom-up. Use the operator's published PUE (Google, Microsoft/Azure) or the industry average from the Uptime Institute survey. State the value.

### 2.6 Source hierarchy (use the highest tier available)
1. Peer-reviewed papers and meta-analyses (e.g. *Science*, *Nature*, *Joule*, *Environmental Science & Technology*, *Journal of Cleaner Production*, *Transportation Research Part D*, arXiv papers with established authors, clearly flagged as preprints)
2. Intergovernmental and governmental bodies, standards and public LCA databases: IEA, EEA, IPCC, UK DESNZ/DEFRA GHG Conversion Factors, ICAO, US EPA, CBS/RIVM, **Agribalyse (ADEME)**, JEC Well-to-Wheels (EU JRC), **ISO 14083**, **GLEC Framework (Smart Freight Centre)**
3. Established NGOs and research groups: Our World in Data, Carbon Trust, The Shift Project (note that its 2019 streaming estimate was later criticised as too high), Ember, Epoch AI, CE Delft, TNO, ICCT, MIT Center for Transportation & Logistics
4. Company and industry-association disclosures: Google, Microsoft, OpenAI, Mistral, GitHub, Anthropic, PostNL, DHL, Thuiswinkel.org. Treat as self-reported.
5. Media and blogs: **only** to point to a tier 1–4 source, never as the source itself.

For contested items (streaming, LLM queries), **cite at least two independent sources** and explain why they differ.

### 2.7 Recency
Prefer sources from **2020 or later**, and from **2024 or later** for anything AI-related. Older figures, such as Google's 2009 per-search figure, may be used when nothing newer exists, but must be flagged with their year.

---

## 3. Items: definitions, functional units and default assumptions

> The agent may refine assumptions, but must record every change in the assumptions register (D5).

### Transport
| ID | Item | Functional unit | Default assumptions | Suggested starting sources |
|----|------|-----------------|---------------------|---------------------------|
| ✈️ | Flight Amsterdam → Geneva | 1 passenger, one way | Great-circle distance ≈ 700 km (verify); economy; direct; add uplift for actual flight path if the method requires. Energy = jet fuel per passenger × ~12 kWh/kg kerosene (cite LHV) | ICAO Carbon Emissions Calculator (fuel burn per pax); DESNZ/DEFRA short-haul factors (with/without RF); EEA; myclimate/atmosfair methodology |
| ⛽️🚗 | Petrol car | 1 car, one way, **road distance** AMS→GVA (≈ 950–1,000 km; verify) | Average EU/NL petrol passenger car, real-world (not WLTP) consumption; 1 occupant. Energy = litres × ~8.9 kWh/L petrol (cite LHV). **Also give per-km and per-passenger (×1 and ×4 occupants) in the dossier** | DESNZ/DEFRA; EEA real-world CO₂ monitoring; ICCT; JEC WTW |
| 🔋🚙 | Electric car | Same trip as ⛽️🚗 | Average EV real-world consumption (kWh/km incl. charging losses) × reference grid | ICCT; EEA; IEA Global EV Outlook; DESNZ/DEFRA |

> **Edge case — "same distance":** flight distance and road distance differ by ~35–40%. Use road distance for cars and flight distance for the flight. Also give **per-km** values (Wh/km and g CO₂e/km) for all three in the dossier so they can be compared like-for-like.

### Logistics
| ID | Item | Functional unit | Default assumptions | Suggested starting sources |
|----|------|-----------------|---------------------|---------------------------|
| 📦 | Online order, next-day home delivery | 1 parcel delivered to the door | **NL webshop + NL warehouse**; total distance travelled by the parcel **≈ 100–200 km** (central 150 km). Typical small parcel (~1–2 kg, shoebox size; state assumption). Chain: warehouse handling → linehaul truck to sorting hub → truck to regional depot → last-mile van to door. Next-day service with a national parcel carrier (PostNL/DHL-type network). Last-mile van mix: diesel vs. electric per current NL fleet share (state source and %). Include a **failed-delivery / re-delivery rate** (state %). **Exclude** production of product and packaging, the customer's own ordering (website visit), and returns. Mention returns in one line, with the NL return rate if easy to source | **Method:** GLEC Framework v3 / ISO 14083 (per-parcel or tonne-km allocation, hub emission intensities). **Factors:** DESNZ/DEFRA freight factors (HGV, van; per vehicle-km and tonne-km); STREAM Goederenvervoer (CE Delft, NL freight emission factors); TNO. **Context:** CE Delft / Thuiswinkel.org studies on e-commerce emissions in NL; peer-reviewed last-mile LCAs (e.g. *Transportation Research Part D*, *Journal of Cleaner Production*); MIT CTL e-commerce vs. retail studies; PostNL and DHL sustainability reports, with per-parcel CO₂ figures flagged as self-reported |

> **Edge cases for 📦:**
> - The 100–200 km is mostly **linehaul** in a well-filled truck (very low per parcel). The **last few km** in a van with many stops usually dominate per-parcel emissions. Show the split per leg in the dossier.
> - Allocate per parcel by **parcels per vehicle trip** for the last mile (stops/parcels per round) and by **weight or volume share (tonne-km)** for linehaul. State which method is used for each leg.
> - **Next-day vs. standard delivery:** if a source shows that next-day lowers vehicle fill rates or adds trips, mention the effect. Do not model it separately unless a source gives a number.
> - **Optional sensitivity in the dossier only:** delivery to a parcel pickup point instead of the door, and an all-electric last mile.

### Food
| ID | Item | Functional unit | Default assumptions | Suggested starting sources |
|----|------|-----------------|---------------------|---------------------------|
| 🍔 | Beef hamburger | 1 burger meal | ~113 g (¼ lb) beef patty + bun + garnish; fries/drink excluded (mention separately) | **CO₂e:** Poore & Nemecek (2018), *Science*, via Our World in Data; WRI. **Energy (CED):** Agribalyse 3.x indicator "energy resources, non-renewable" (MJ/kg); peer-reviewed beef LCAs reporting CED; ecoinvent-based studies |
| 🍗 | Chicken | 1 portion | ~150 g poultry meat | Same as above |
| 🧀 | Cheese | 1 portion | ~50 g cheese (state whether hard cheese) | Same as above; RIVM/Dutch food LCA database as cross-check |

> - **CO₂e:** use the **global median** from Poore & Nemecek as the central value and the reported 10th–90th percentile as the range. Note that beef from beef herds differs strongly from beef from dairy herds.
> - **Energy:** report **production energy only** (CED, MJ/kg → Wh per portion). If CED for the exact product is unavailable, use the closest product and say so. If sources mix renewable and non-renewable CED, use **total CED** and note it.
> - **Do not report or mention food calories.**
> - CO₂e and energy may come from different studies. Note this, and check that the system boundaries roughly match (cradle-to-retail).

### Everyday digital
| ID | Item | Functional unit | Default assumptions | Suggested starting sources |
|----|------|-----------------|---------------------|---------------------------|
| 🔍 | Google search (no AI) | 1 query | Server side + network + share of user device for ~a few seconds | Google (2009) 0.0003 kWh/search statement; newer independent estimates; note that AI Overviews change this |
| 📺 | Video streaming | 1 hour | HD on a laptop or TV over home Wi-Fi/fixed broadband; include device, network and data centre | IEA (Kamiya, 2020 revised analysis); Carbon Trust (2021) white paper on video streaming; note The Shift Project controversy |
| 🎧 | Music streaming | 1 hour | Smartphone or laptop, standard quality; include device, network and data centre | Peer-reviewed estimates if available (e.g. Brennan & Devine on recorded music); otherwise derive (§4.4) |
| 🧑‍💻 | Human computer work | 1 hour | Laptop (~15–30 W) + one external monitor (~20–30 W), local work only; **exclude** cloud services; embodied emissions as a caveat | Manufacturer product environmental reports (Dell, Apple, Lenovo), Energy Star data |
| 🧑‍🧑‍🧒‍🧒 | Teams meeting + recording | 1 meeting-hour | **4 participants**, cameras on, each on laptop + Wi-Fi; plus cloud recording stored for **1 year**. Report total per meeting and per participant | Obringer et al. (2021), *Resources, Conservation & Recycling*; Microsoft sustainability disclosures; derive storage via §4.4 |

### AI and compute
| ID | Item | Functional unit | Default assumptions | Suggested starting sources |
|----|------|-----------------|---------------------|---------------------------|
| 🤖 | Simple LLM chat query | 1 prompt + 1 response | Frontier chat model; ~100 input tokens, ~500 output tokens; no web search, no reasoning mode | Epoch AI (2025) ChatGPT energy estimate; Google (2025) Gemini per-prompt technical paper; Mistral LCA (2025); Jegham et al. (2025) "How Hungry is AI?"; Luccioni et al.; OpenAI CEO statement (flag as self-reported) |
| 🦾 | Agent task, medium | 1 task | GitHub Copilot agent / Claude Code; **~10 min wall-clock, ~10–30 tool calls**. Token profile to be researched (§4.2): expect most input tokens to be **cached context re-reads** | Derived from 🤖 per-token energy + token counts from public usage reports/telemetry (e.g. Claude Code `/cost` output examples, Anthropic/GitHub docs, developer write-ups) |
| 🦾🦾 | Agent task, large | 1 task | **~1 h wall-clock, 100+ tool calls**; same method | Same |
| 🗄️ | GitHub Actions CI | 5 min of runtime | Standard GitHub-hosted Linux runner (verify current vCPU/RAM spec for private vs. public repos); CPU-only; medium load (~50% utilisation) | GitHub runner specs; Cloud Carbon Footprint methodology (per-vCPU W, per-GB RAM W, PUE); Azure region grid intensity |

---

## 4. Calculation methods (use when no direct source exists)

### 4.1 General
```
Energy (Wh) = Power (W) × Time (h) × PUE (if data centre)
CO₂e (g)    = Energy (kWh) × Grid intensity (g CO₂e/kWh)
J           = Wh × 3,600
Primary energy (Wh) = Final electricity (Wh) × primary-energy factor
                    = Final fuel energy (Wh) × (1 + well-to-tank uplift)
Food: Energy (Wh per portion) = CED (MJ/kg) × portion (kg) × 1,000,000 / 3,600
```

### 4.2 LLM / agent tasks
1. From the 🤖 sources, establish **energy per 1,000 output tokens** and, where available, **per 1,000 input tokens**. Input/prefill is much cheaper per token than output/decode, and **cached** input cheaper still. Document the ratio used.
2. Build a **token profile** for 🦾 and 🦾🦾:
   - number of model turns (≈ number of tool calls + 1)
   - context size per turn (grows over the session)
   - split into fresh input, cached input and output tokens
   - cross-check against real-world reported session token counts or costs. Price-to-token conversion is acceptable as a sanity check.
3. Energy = Σ(tokens × energy per token type) × PUE.
4. Add the **tool-execution side** (local laptop running tests, builds, etc.) using §4.1 and the 🧑‍💻 figure × duration.
5. Report a **low / central / high** scenario. The spread will be large; that is acceptable and should be stated.

### 4.3 CI runner
```
Power = vCPUs × (W_min + util × (W_max − W_min)) + RAM_GB × W_per_GB
Energy = Power × (5/60 h) × PUE
```
Use Cloud Carbon Footprint coefficients for Azure and state them.

### 4.4 Network and storage
- Network: use a **kWh/GB** figure from a recent peer-reviewed source (e.g. Aslan et al. 2018, or newer). Note that marginal network energy per GB is contested, and power-model approaches (Carbon Trust/IEA) give much lower values. State which approach is used.
- Storage, for the Teams recording: GB stored × kWh/GB·year (cite source) × replication factor.

### 4.5 Parcel delivery (📦)
Prefer a published per-parcel figure for NL (CE Delft, Thuiswinkel.org, peer-reviewed or carrier data) **and** cross-check it with this bottom-up build, following GLEC/ISO 14083:

```
Per leg:
  Linehaul (truck):   energy = vehicle energy per km × km ÷ parcels on board
                      (or tonne-km × energy per tonne-km, by parcel weight)
  Last mile (van):    energy = (van energy per km × round km) ÷ parcels delivered per round
  Hubs / warehouse:   energy = hub energy intensity per parcel (GLEC default or source)
Total final energy = Σ legs × (1 + failed-delivery rate on the last-mile leg)
CO₂e  = diesel litres × tank-to-wheel factor
      + electricity (EV vans, hubs) × NL grid intensity
```
- Use a weighted diesel/electric mix for the last-mile van (state the share and source).
- Report the per-leg split (warehouse, linehaul, hub/depot, last mile), so the reader can see where emissions concentrate.
- Report low / central / high: low = 100 km, high van fill, electric last mile; high = 200 km, low fill, diesel last mile, higher failed-delivery rate.

### 4.6 Footprint of this report (D6, nice-to-have)
**Log from the very start of the work.** This cannot be reconstructed reliably afterwards.

1. **Record during the work:**
   - total model turns / LLM calls
   - input, cached-input and output tokens, read from the agent's usage/cost metadata where available, otherwise estimated
   - number of tool calls, split by type (web searches, page fetches, code runs)
   - total wall-clock time of the agent session
   - human time spent briefing and reviewing (estimate, in hours)
2. **Calculate:**
   - LLM inference: same per-token method and factors as 🦾/🦾🦾 (§4.2)
   - web searches: × 🔍 figure (or 🤖 figure if the searches are AI-assisted; state which)
   - code execution / sandbox: §4.1 with an assumed small VM power, × duration
   - human computer time: × 🧑‍💻 figure
3. **Report:** low / central / high for energy (Wh) and CO₂e (g), plus the token counts and tool-call counts used. Add one comparison sentence, e.g. "producing this report ≈ N Google searches ≈ X % of one burger".
4. **Place it as the last footnote of the report**, clearly labelled as a self-estimate using the report's own method.

---

## 5. Hypothesis ranges for sanity checks (NOT results)

Use these **only** to catch unit errors, e.g. a Wh/kWh mix-up. If a result falls outside the range, double-check it; if it holds, explain why. Energy ranges are **final energy** for transport/logistics/digital and **production energy (CED)** for food.

| Item | Expected energy ballpark | Expected CO₂e ballpark |
|------|-------------------------|-----------------------|
| ✈️ AMS→GVA, 1 pax | ~150–400 kWh | ~100–250 kg |
| ⛽️🚗 ~1,000 km, 1 car | ~500–800 kWh | ~150–250 kg |
| 🔋🚙 ~1,000 km | ~150–250 kWh | ~20–80 kg (NL grid) |
| 🍔 burger | ~0.5–5 kWh | ~3–12 kg |
| 🍗 chicken portion | ~0.5–3 kWh | ~0.5–2 kg |
| 🧀 cheese portion | ~0.3–2 kWh | ~0.5–2 kg |
| 📦 parcel, next-day, ~150 km | ~0.2–3 kWh | ~50–1,000 g |
| 🔍 search | ~0.1–1 Wh | ~0.1–1 g |
| 📺 1 h video | ~20–200 Wh | ~20–100 g |
| 🎧 1 h music | ~2–50 Wh | ~1–50 g |
| 🧑‍💻 1 h computer work | ~30–70 Wh | ~5–30 g |
| 🧑‍🧑‍🧒‍🧒 1 h Teams (4 people) | ~150–600 Wh | ~50–500 g |
| 🤖 chat query | ~0.1–5 Wh | ~0.03–5 g |
| 🦾 / 🦾🦾 agent tasks | unknown: key research gap | expect 1–3 orders of magnitude above 🤖 |
| 🗄️ 5 min CI | ~1–10 Wh | ~0.5–5 g |
| 📄 this report (D6) | unknown | expect roughly the 🦾🦾 range or higher |

## 6. Dossier template (one section per item, D1)

```markdown
### [Emoji] [Item name]
**Functional unit:** …
**Boundary:** operational / life-cycle / with-or-without RF
**Energy basis:** Final / Primary (CED); primary-energy factor used: …
**Assumptions:** bullet list (also copied to D5)
**Sources:**
1. [Author/Org, Year. Title. Publisher. URL. Accessed YYYY-MM-DD] — tier X — key figure quoted verbatim
2. …
**Calculation:** step-by-step with units (for 📦: per leg)
**Result:**
- Energy: central A Wh = B J (range …); primary energy C Wh
- CO₂e: central X g (range Y–Z)
**Confidence:** High / Medium / Low, with one-line justification
**Caveats & disagreements:** …
```

## 7. Comparison table spec (D2)

Sort rows by **central g CO₂e per functional unit, descending**.

| Column | Content |
|--------|---------|
| Item | Emoji + short label |
| Functional unit | e.g. "1 pax, AMS→GVA one-way", "1 parcel, next-day, ~150 km", "1 burger", "1 hour", "1 query" |
| Absolute rate | Intensity in the natural unit, e.g. Wh/km and g CO₂e/km; g CO₂e per parcel-km; MJ/kg and kg CO₂e/kg food; W; Wh/1k tokens |
| Energy basis | Final / Primary (CED) |
| Energy per unit (final) | Wh **and** J (for food: production CED; marked) |
| Energy per unit (primary) | Wh: the like-for-like comparison column for **all** rows |
| CO₂e per unit, central | g CO₂e |
| CO₂e range | low–high |
| Order of magnitude | 10ⁿ g CO₂e and 10ⁿ Wh |
| Equivalent in 🤖 queries | central CO₂e ÷ 🤖 central CO₂e (an intuitive yardstick) |
| Confidence | H / M / L |
| Key source(s) | Short citation keys pointing to D4 |

Below the table, add:
- 3–5 **key takeaways** in plain language, e.g. "one burger ≈ N agent tasks", "one parcel ≈ N hours of video", and the energy-vs-CO₂e gap for food
- The **grid-intensity, primary-energy-factor and RF assumptions** in one line each
- **Last footnote:** the footprint of this report (D6)

## 8. Workflow for the agent

0. **Start logging your own usage** (tokens, tool calls, time) for D6 (§4.6).
1. **Set the global parameters** in §2 (grid intensity, PUE, RF factor, primary-energy factors) and record them in D5.
2. **Research the items in the order of §3.** For each one, follow the dossier template. Expect transport and food CO₂e to be quick; parcel delivery (per-leg build), food energy (CED) and the digital and AI items take the most effort.
3. **Run the calculations** from §4 where needed. Show the working and keep a spreadsheet or script so the numbers can be reproduced.
4. **Check results against the ranges in §5** and investigate any outliers.
5. **Compile D2–D5** from the dossier.
6. **Close the usage log and compute D6.** Add it as the last footnote.
7. **Self-review** against the acceptance checklist (§9).

---

## 9. Acceptance checklist

- [ ] All 16 items present with central value + range, for **both energy and CO₂e**
- [ ] Food energy = production energy (CED) only; no food calories anywhere
- [ ] Energy basis (final / primary) labelled per row; primary-energy column filled for all rows with factors stated
- [ ] Every number has a cited source or a derivation whose inputs are all cited
- [ ] At least two sources for 📺, 🤖, 🔍, ✈️, 📦
- [ ] Grid intensity, PUE, primary-energy factors and RF assumptions stated once globally and applied consistently
- [ ] Units consistent (g CO₂e; Wh and J); no Wh/kWh, kg/g or MJ/kWh mix-ups
- [ ] Per-km values given for all transport modes; occupancy stated for cars
- [ ] 📦: per-leg split shown; allocation method, van fuel mix and failed-delivery rate stated; product and packaging production excluded
- [ ] Food reported on a life-cycle basis, with this noted in the table
- [ ] Agent-task token profile documented and cross-checked
- [ ] Self-reported company figures flagged as such
- [ ] All URLs resolve; access dates recorded
- [ ] Takeaways are proportionate and do not overstate precision
- [ ] *(Nice-to-have)* D6 footprint-of-this-report footnote included, with the token and tool-call counts it is based on

---

## 10. Out of scope

- Food calories / nutritional energy
- 📦: production of the product and of the packaging material; the customer's ordering session; returns (one-line mention only); comparison with a trip to a physical shop (may be mentioned in one line if a source gives it)
- Water use, land use and biodiversity: may be mentioned in one line where striking (e.g. beef, AI data-centre water), but not quantified
- Model **training** emissions: mention as a caveat only, possibly as an amortised-per-query figure if a reputable source offers one
- Offsetting and carbon-credit claims
- Precision beyond the order of magnitude
````
:::

**The new 📦 row:**
- **What's counted:** only the delivery chain. That covers the warehouse, the trucks between sorting centres, the hubs, and the last-mile van to the door, including deliveries that fail and have to be repeated. Making the product and the packaging is left out, as you asked.
  - I also left out returns, your own time ordering online, and any comparison with a trip to a shop. The agent may mention these in one line but won't calculate them.
- **What the agent should show:** the emissions for each step of the route.
  - Most of the 100–200 km is in well-filled trucks, so it adds little per parcel. The last few kilometres in a van that stops at many addresses usually account for most of the emissions.
  - Seeing the steps separately should make the result easier to understand.
- **How it's calculated:** the agent follows the GLEC Framework / ISO 14083, the standard method for working out emissions per shipment. It also checks the result against published per-parcel figures for the Netherlands where those exist (CE Delft, Thuiswinkel.org, PostNL/DHL).

**Choices to check before handing it off:**
- **Parcel size:** I assumed a small parcel of about 1–2 kg, roughly shoebox size.
- **Van fuel mix:** the agent uses the current Dutch share of diesel versus electric delivery vans rather than picking one type.
- **Expected range in §5:** I put a parcel at roughly 50–1,000 g CO₂e. Like the other ranges, that's my own wide guess for catching unit mistakes, not a sourced figure.

Now I'm working out the last-mile van share: diesel comes to about 0.13 kg and 0.5 kWh per parcel, the electric van to roughly 36 g. Weighted 70/30 and adjusted for failed deliveries, that gives about 109 g CO2e and 0.41 kWh per parcel. Next I'm turning to the linehaul leg of 150 km by articulated truck, where I'm estimating about 0.9 kg CO2e per km and roughly 3.5 kWh per km of diesel.

Now I'm estimating the linehaul leg on a per-parcel basis: roughly 1,100 parcels per truck over 150 km, which puts the truck share at around 0.12 kg CO2e and 0.48 kWh per parcel. That feels high, so I'll cross-check it against a tonne-kilometre approach.

Now I'm settling the linehaul leg: an articulated truck at roughly 0.9 kg CO2e per km over 150 km, shared across about 1,500 parcels, comes to around 90 g per parcel. That puts it close to the last-mile figure of roughly 109 g, so the two legs are comparable rather than one dominating, which I'll report as is.

I'm adding up the warehouse and hub share now, which comes to roughly 0.15 kWh per parcel, and I'm not sure of that number, so I'll flag it as uncertain.

Now I'm adding up the parcel footprint: roughly 109 g from last-mile delivery, about 90 g from the linehaul truck legs, and around 40 g from warehouse and hub electricity and heating, for a central total near 240 g CO2e. I'm flagging the range as wide, about 100 to 600 g, and noting that I'm not sure which published study to cite for this.

Now I'm checking the digital side, starting with Google's 2009 figure of about 0.3 Wh per search, and I'll be flagging that I haven't verified a per-parcel figure from a published Dutch study in this run.

I'm checking the digital figure against Google's 2009 number of about 0.2 g CO2 per search, and I'm setting a central estimate of around 0.3 Wh with a range down to 0.04 Wh. At roughly 400 g per kWh for data-centre load, that comes to about 0.1 g CO2 per query, and I'll set it beside Google's more recent prompt estimate for comparison.

Now I'm estimating the streaming side: a Carbon Trust figure of about 55 g CO2e per hour for European viewing, and an IEA figure of roughly 0.077 kWh and 36 g per hour globally. Taking a central energy of around 80–100 Wh and the Dutch grid intensity, I land near 40 g per hour with a range of about 20–100 g.

Now I'm checking the music streaming side, estimating the phone and network energy for about 72 MB per hour at roughly 0.01 to 0.03 kWh per GB, and I'm not sure which device-level figure to trust, so I'll keep the range wide and flag it.

Now I'm working out the device side for a laptop-based hour: a 20 W laptop plus a 25 W monitor is about 45 Wh, which at the Dutch grid comes to roughly 12 g CO2e, with a range of about 7 to 20 g depending on the setup.

Now I'm checking the videoconferencing figure, which Obringer et al. put at roughly 150–1,000 g CO2e per hour, though that seems to rest on high network energy assumptions. I'll try a bottom-up estimate: four participants, each with a laptop around 45 Wh plus video data at HD rates.

Now I'm estimating the video call footprint bottom-up: four participants at roughly 60 Wh each, mostly from laptops and about 0.7 GB per hour of video data, comes to around 240 Wh per hour before adding recording and storage.

Now I'm checking the digital side against the video call numbers. Obringer's upper figure sits at the top of the range, so I'll keep it as the high-end case. Now I'm comparing the AI query estimates: Epoch's 0.3 Wh, Google's 0.24 Wh and roughly 0.03 g on a market-based basis, Altman's self-reported 0.34 Wh, and Mistral's 1.14 g of CO2.

Now I'm weighing the Mistral figure of 1.14 g CO2e per 400-token response, which its lifecycle study says includes infrastructure, and I'm treating my own central estimate of 0.3 Wh with a range of 0.1 to 3 Wh as the working number. At a grid intensity of about 0.4 kg per kWh, that gives roughly 0.12 g CO2 per query, with a range of 0.03 to 1.2 g.

Now I'm settling the CI runner side: GitHub's hosted Linux runners differ by repo type, with public repos getting more CPU and memory than private ones, so I'll note the spec difference and flag the exact current figures as something to double-check.

Now I'm working through the CI runner energy: at 50% load, four vCPUs and 16 GB come to roughly 1.5 Wh for a five-minute job, which at the Dutch grid works out to about 0.6 g CO2e.

Now I'm estimating my own session footprint: roughly eight model calls with around 60k input tokens each, mostly cached, and about 60k output tokens. Weighting output, fresh input, and cached input differently gives an estimate

Thuiswinkel.org heeft in 2018 de Bewust Bezorgd Simulator geïntroduceerd waarmee webwinkels hun consumenten kunnen laten zien wat het effect van hun bestelling is; welke uitstoot veroorzaakt hun bestelling? Met deze kennis kan de consument bewustere keuzes maken. Onze adviezen zijn:  Houd bij nieuwbouwwijken rekening met een veilige en duurzame thuisbezorging; zorg voor voldoende laad- en losplekken en afhaalpunten.  Stimuleer kleine zero-emissievoertuigen in de bezorging. [...] dat in een busje past. Thuiswinkel.org heeft namens de sector de brancheverduurzamingsplannen gepubliceerd waarin wordt gefocust op duurzamer verpakken met meer herbruikbare verpakkingen, minder opvulmateriaal, op maat gemaakte verpakkingen in automatische verpakkingsmachines en gerecycled verpakkingsmateriaal. Als er minder opvulmateriaal en lucht in pakketten zit, kunnen vervoerders meer pakketten per rit meenemen of een kleiner voertuig inzetten en passen meer pakketten door de brievenbus.\nSource: retailinsiders.nl\n\n## Highlights\n\n### Highlight 1 [Relevance: 0.00]\n\n```text\nDeze factsheet is tot stand gekomen in het kader van de samenwerking tussen de Tweede Kamer, de KNAW, NWO, VSNU en de jonge Akademie cvxvxcvgrafische effect van tekst op deze plek. 1 september 2021 Auteurs: Dr. Walther Ploos van Amstel, lector Stadslogistiek aan de Hogeschool van Amsterdam, Dr. Jesse Weltevreden, lector Digital Commerce aan de Hogeschool van Amsterdam, Dr. Hans Quak,lector Smart Cities and Logistics Breda University of Applied Sciences & senior scientist bij TNO, Meike Hopman MSc, research consultant Logistics bij TNO BEZORGECONOMIE: DE EFFECTEN OP MOBILITEIT, MILIEU EN EN LEEFOMGEVING leefomgeving Inleiding Onlinewinkelen is populair. Tussen 2010 en 2019 zijn de onlinebestedingen in Nederland volgens brancheorganisatie Thuiswinkel.org gestegen van circa 10 miljard tot bijna 26 miljard euro.1 In 2020 hebben de coronamaatregelen geleid tot een groei van 40 tot 60% in het aantal onlineaankopen (data CBS over 2020 en 2021). De groei vlak in het tweede kwartaal van 2021 af. De verwachting is dat ook na de coronacrisis sprake blijft van een verdere verschuiving van fysiek winkelen naar onlinewinkelen; een verwachte groei per jaar van 15% bij multichannelwinkels en 7,5% bij pure webwinkels.2 Daarmee rijst de vraag wat de effecten van de bezorgeconomie zijn voor onder andere mobiliteit, logistiek, het milieu en de verkeersveiligheid. De centrale vraagstelling van deze bijdrage is: Deze bijdrage geeft inzicht in de omvang, trends en effecten van de bezorgeconomie in Nederland. Aan de hand van vijf aspecten wordt de invloed op mobiliteit, milieu en leefomgeving beschreven: volumegroei, afhaalopties, type voertuigen, verpakkingen en retouren. De bezorgmarkt telt vijf belangrijke segmenten: pakketten van webwinkels (zowel business-to-consumer als business-to-business3), pakketten local-for-local (van lokale leveranciers naar lokale klanten), bezorging met twee bezorgers van grotere producten (zoals meubels en witgoed), bezorging van levensmiddelen en maaltijdbezorging. Daarnaast is er nog een klein netwerk waarin consumenten onderling versturen (zoals bij tweedehandsverkopen via Marktplaats en Vinted). Volumegroei Samenvatting: dagelijks zijn 90.000 tot 130.000 bezorgers onderweg voor onlinebestellingen. De kosten van bezorgen zijn 6 tot 8 miljard euro per jaar. Het effect op CO2-uitstoot en lokale luchtkwaliteit is beperkt. Met meer bestellingen neemt het aantal voertuigen voor pakketten niet evenredig toe. Dit gebeurt wel bij levensmiddelen- en tweemansbezorging en maaltijd- en flitsbezorging. Over het aantal leveringen, voertuigen en bezorgers zijn beperkte statistieken. Op basis van data van webwinkels, brancheorganisaties, CBS, ACM en de Topsector Logistiek kan wel een redelijke schatting worden gemaakt van de omvang van de bezorgmarkt (Tabel 1). De maaltijdbezorging en flitsbezorging worden verder niet besproken, maar worden qua problematiek met thuisbezorging in steden steeds belangrijker.
